 (D)lirium
 Review: Info on the Russian producer Curity is shrouded in mystery but this EP on Acquit is enough to suggest whoever they are, they know how to craft seriously sophisticated techno. 'Synthetic Mind' is deep and cavernous with deft pads and beautiful chords swirling over the dusty drum work. 'Standart Routine' picks up the pace with bumpier drums but still warm atmospheres and 'Ephemeral' is another inviting, immersive deep techno sound with gorgeous synth work. '(D)lirium' shuts down with a classy touch of Motor City steel. … Read more

 Ricardo Angeles - "Kids Playing" (6:26)
 Review: For their seventh vinyl releases, Peaces - the Russian imprint previously known as Peace Symphonies - has offered up a split EP featuring tracks from two of its numerous affiliated artists. Daniel handles side A, pairing the softly spun, gently sunny and shuffling deep house positivity of 'From Here To There in Under a Second' with the deliciously dreamy, piano-splashed, breakdown-rich deep tech-house beauty of 'The World is Full of Light'. Ricardo Angeles steps up on side B, first unveiling the deep, dreamy progressive house retro-futurism of 'Changes', before doffing a cap to the immersive, dancefloor-ready sound worlds of the popular All Day I Dream label. … Read more

 Guardian Of The Green (3:44)
 [link: https://www.juno.co.uk/MP3/SF1179415-01-02-01.mp3] [audio: https://www.juno.co.uk/MP3/SF1179415-01-02-01.mp3] 
 Celestial Bodies (3:08)
 Review: Ghanaian producer and songwriter Delasi and Tokyo-based experimental dub poppers TAMTAM join forces for an immersive two-tracker that pulls you in and then envelops you in the thick textures of its tones. Opening on the deep grooves of the title number, it's a slow, building vibe, wispy vocals sounding like the haze of spliff smoke dissipating through the darkness of a room made only for lunging low ends to be felt at full force. Flip it to find something altogether sweeter, but no less tripped-out. A blissful combination of harmonious loop and distorted woodwind refrains, words flowing in and out of consciousness, as though we're catching an aural glimpse of song from elsewhere, arriving in our earshot on the gentle breeze of a warm afternoon.

 Can't Help Me
 [link: https://www.juno.co.uk/MP3/SF1169073-01-02-02.mp3] [audio: https://www.juno.co.uk/MP3/SF1169073-01-02-02.mp3] 
 Pressure
 Review: Run between Barcelona and Berlin by Moon&Mann aka Da Silva and DJ Katah, MM Discos has been serving up high-grade treats since the tail end of 2015. Here they follow-up successful recent missives from Nic Jalusi and Daichi with a Balearic-minded - but undoubtedly dancefloor-ready - five-tracker courtesy of Spencer Terry's Desert Island Disco collective. They first doff a cap to the mid-tempo warmth, sunshine and synthesiser colours of 1980s South African bubblegum on the gorgeous 'Ifuna', before breaking up the beats a little on the dub disco-goes-kwaito excellence of 'Ikuna'. 'Iyo' fuses the low-end pulse of Italo-disco with summery South African synth-pop, 'Can't Help Me' is a dreamy and loved-up Balearic house masterpiece, and 'Pressure' is an authentic-sounding chunk of late-80s kwaito brilliance. A genuine must-check EP. … Read more

 Loom
 Review: Arthur Hnatek's drum kit has developed melodic ambitions, and frankly, who's going to tell it no? 'Undertow', the Zurich-based Swiss musician's four-track Hessle Audio debut, began as freewheeling drum improvisations, which were subsequently processed until the percussion could handle the harmony as well. And blimey, the title track: great, heart-swelling electronic billows above drums that seem to have independently articulated elbows. There's real feeling in all the rhythmic fussiness. … Read more

 Fugue - "Interlagos" (7:03)
 Review: Hi-hats become flange-swirled tuned elements; snares spit long sprinklets of liquid tail and melt into the phase haze. These ambient electro cuts are like cocaine. In 1994 UK emotive techno pusher Nuron/Fugue (Nurmad Jusat) singled himself out as a like mind (not surprisingly - this isn't a duo project despite appearances) and ran with the concept for the eventual six-track series' name, Likemind. Now the way-back split EP now gets a reissue, and presumably hearkens others to be reissued to come, through Blueprint Archives. The Fugue tunes are certainly a lot more relaxing, spelling out quite clearly the differences in intent and outcome between the two monikers. … Read more

 Rougail Space Program - "Late Nite Gaze" (4:55)
 Review: Weirdies is the freshly minted label of Parisian Jon Beige, who has already turned up on Global Warming and R.W.A. and spent a spell as a Rinse France resident. Its first vinyl is a sampler with some serious company following a great run of digi-only releases. Side A is for the floor: rougail space program's 'Tamarin Sex' and ECLT's woozy 'Consciousness', which samples the late Genesis P-Orridge, are both overdriven juke and techno and bass collisions with hyper real synths and chore surfaces Side B chills out with the sweet drift of 'u & me' and the Basic Channel-ish 'Late Nite Gaze'. A brilliant wax debut. … Read more

 Two-level Sleep (3:03)
 Review: Yekaterinburg producer Shine Grooves returns to Gost Zvuk with Pyshma, a work that favours atmosphere over pure dance floor functionalism. Shimmering electronics, lo-fi loops and fleeting melodic fragments drift throughout and give that familiar feeling of half-remembered places and late-night thoughts. Subtle rhythms emerge and dissolve beneath the haze, not least on the enchanting 'Ethereal Peepaa ' with its Eastern-licked melodies, or the icy-cold feel of 'Vid Iz Okna' and more sustained, suspensory ambience of 'Zaputannaya'. Pyshma is one of those albums that draws you in and, once you're fully locked in reveals a ton of detail. … Read more

 Tasm (bonus track) (5:15)
 Review: Scottish techno's Alex Smoke hasn't been heard from for a while so it's nice he's back under his Zar alias for EP3, a collaboration with Void Control that centres on heavy rhythms and percussion. 'Geodesic' is a lurching, broken rhythm laden with organic percussion and pained synth chords which Void Control remixes into something darker, more pronounced and textured. He then offers the off-balance dub depths of 'Temporary' which Zar turns into something more experimental and packed with scratchy texture, detune notes and eerie bells. Stumbling dub closer 'Tasm' is a bonus track that has previously only done the rounds on dubplate. … Read more

 Procissao Dos Mortos (0:31)
 Review: Born in Lisbon in 1999, Ana Lua Caiano began her career during lockdown, writing a song a week, then played WOMEX and Eurosonic, which helped her establish a wider audience. Her 2024 debut, Vou Ficar Neste Quadrado was one of the year's most acclaimed folk-electronic records and on album two she brings piano, bagpipes, adufe and bombo, and even sewing machines as percussion. The title, 'Slowly, for life is short', was her grandfather's refrain and across the record songs wrestle with mortality, emigration and work. From 'Uma Vida a Menos' to 'Come, Come, Cresce' her voice is by turns fragile, ritualistic and feverish and somehow results in an ancient and thrilling listen. … Read more

 Seoul 03 (feat Amelie)
 [link: https://www.juno.co.uk/MP3/SF1183927-01-02-03.mp3] [audio: https://www.juno.co.uk/MP3/SF1183927-01-02-03.mp3] 
 La Porte De L'Enfer
 Review: Earring presents a time capsule of work made in their 20s and 30s, here pieced together as an 'audio essay' responding to the ideas of Danish philosopher Soren Kierkegaard. Also known as the Father of Existentialism, the academic theorist posited that "anxiety is the vertigo of freedom", and across seven tracks it becomes increasingly easy to understand how that has been interpreted sonically. Jittering synth lines giving way to unapologetic four-to-the-floor grooves that feel unstable and vulnerable but also headstrong and determined. Like re-entering Earth's atmosphere in a homemade rocket that will survive, but with a good amount of damage. Spoken word messages with echoed ambient refrains. Uncompromising peak time techno. Haunting sound waves. A record that feels uneasy, but not oppressive.

 Ar Fresco (feat Sreya & Rachel Kitchlew) (3:51)
 Review: Second time around for Golf Trip, a project that draws together such disparate artists as Norwegian producer Olefunken, Swiss vocalist Sreya and British harpist Rachel Kitchlew. The fluid collective's second full-length (the first landed back in 2022), it's reportedly "built around the idea of escape". It's loosely rooted in Balearica and dreamy, jazz-funk-flecked indie-pop, with a warming and hard-to-pigeonhole sound that's rich in slow-motion rhythms, heady basslines, dream-pop-adjacent guitars, soft-rock sax solos and warming, tactile synth chords. It sounds like the kind of thing designed to be listened to late at night and early in the morning, with opaque colours, deliciously huggable grooves and dewy-eyed vocals. … Read more

 Ende (5:18)
 Review: Hong Kong-born, Utrecht-based Kiana Li dropped her first records in 2018 and has since taken her drum & bass onto Metalheadz, XL and Objekt's Kapsela. Her NTS residency is called Field Research, so launching a label of that name earlier this year, to free up her "emotionally-entangled music", seems like an obvious move. Great Jobs is her debut album, and one that swaps pure introspection for more pronounced personality: dub, post-industrial and post-punk all form the foundations from which she explores experiential sounds, glitchy patterns and hyperactive arrangements that reflect our overstimulated modern-day minds. Her phrase is an "inner-world soundclash", and it fits. … Read more

 Review: Producer Vegyn and poet Francis Hornsby Ckark have been working together for Headache for three years, offering up the 21st century trip-hop LPs The Head Hurts But The Heart Knows The Truth (2023) and Thank You For Almost Everything (2025). Last year, they turned their hands to live performance for the first time. It's this aspect of their ongoing collective story that's celebrated by In One Ear and Out The Other, a recording of their first ever performance at Potato Head Beach Club in Bali. A combination of their own beats, samples and vocals with elements of other people's recordings (hence the hush-hush white label release), it's a fine, atmospheric snapshot of their unique musical world. … Read more

 Distant Light
 Review: Amour, Jamie 'Jimpster' Odell's fourth studio album, should be considered a key staging post in the long-serving Essex artist's career. It captures the moment when the Freerange co-founder began to embrace deep house after years exploring downtempo and nu-jazz sounds. Here reissued on vinyl to mark the set's 20th birthday, it remains a lovingly crafted and superbly realised set. There are atmospheric and immersive low-tempo numbers, of course - see the jazzy and drowsy head-nod 'In An Analogue Way', the slo-mo jazz-house-soul of 'Slippin' and the warming nu-jazz lusciousness of 'Secret Sun' - but also oodles of club-ready workouts, with highlights including mutant hip-house gem 'Left to Right' (featuring rapper Capitol A), the ultra-deep shuffle of 'Seventh Wave' and the gorgeously immersive and emotive loveliness of 'I Want It'. … Read more

 Classic (2:38)
 Review: Four Dusseldorf musicians turned soundcheck doodles into a debut that Die Zeit called "avant-garde that's fun right from the start." Thomas Klein, Andreas Reihse, Stefan Schneider and Detlef Weinrich laid most of it down in one or two takes at Berlin's old Milchhof when they were reportedly still buzzing from a gig at Eimer, where the audience watched through a hole in the floor. Krautrock lineage runs through it, with shades of Neu! and Kraftwerk in the grooves. 'Reflections' even snuck onto MTV back in the day with chiming keys softening the motorik grind. Three decades on, it's still an album well worth hearing. … Read more

 Divers Dub Long
 Review: Something of a global meeting of experimental dub minds here, as Japanese ambient dub explorer Mystica Tribe (Taka Noda, who has been releasing music under the alias for 15 years) and Scottish contemporary Scopeotaku (a Glaswegian artist known for his DIY, self-released cassettes). First and foremost, it's a hugely successful and entertaining collaboration, with the pair blending melodic, Balearic-adjacent motifs, ambient pads and sun-bright IDM electronics with deep, dub-wise rhythms, exotic Sitar flourishes and nods aplenty to digi-dub and lovers rock. We're particularly enjoying the enveloping, layered bliss of 'Human Condition', the deep ambient dub of 'Lobely Robot' and the enveloping excellence of 'Divers Dub Long', but it's all impeccable.

 [link: https://www.juno.co.uk/MP3/SF1179029-01-02-04.mp3] [audio: https://www.juno.co.uk/MP3/SF1179029-01-02-04.mp3] 
 Divers Dub Long
 Review: A first joint release from Japanese dub experimentalist Mystica Tribe (real name Taka Noda) and Scottish brother-in-bass Scopeataku, a Glaswegian producer best known for his scuzzy, self-released EPs. Across eight tracks, the pair do a terrific job in fusing deep, heady sub-bass, shuffling beats and digi-dub style melodic stabs with elements of sun-soaked ambient, Augustus Pablo style Melodica, slowly unfurling melodies and the kind of picturesque, summery sonics more often associated with Balearic beats. It's a genuinely fine album all told, with the duo doing a great job in melding their dub-wise inclination with exotic, evocative and emotion-rich blasts of sunset-ready musicality.

 Soley - "Bad Dream"
 Review: While you don't have to be a sound-bore to enjoy In-akustik's Selected Audiophile Music series, the label founded it on the principle of showcasing "outstandingly recorded music" from across the musical spectrum, adding high-grade mastering to ensure exceptional reproduction over even modest home set-ups. The music contained on this second volume naturally sounds good, but is also by and large wonderfully composed, produced and performed. For proof, check the heartfelt, haunting gentleness of CATT's 'Wild Heart (acoustic)', the head-nodding hip-hop soul of Son Little's 'Mad About You', the flash-fried psych-funk of Snarky Puppy ('Mean Green'), the contemporary jazz-fusion breeziness of Philippe Saisse's 'The City That Never Sleeps' and the crystalline ambient of Trentemoller ('Miss You'). … Read more

 Take Your Time
 Like A Sundial
 All Rise
 I Ain't Hiding These Blues
 Review: English producer Goshawk aka Matt Rivem is named after a medium-to-large bird of prey from Eurasia, though we're not sure it has that much baring on his sound. He's a regular on labels like Lumberjacks in Hell and Bristol imprint Boogie Cafe, but here drops a 12" on the Vinyl Only label. The tracks all offer his trademark blend of deep grooves and atmospheric synths with nods to garage, mid-90s US house, late 90s French house and the free party sound that was represented so well on the Nottingham DiY Discs label. Another doozy from the East Midlands man. … Read more
